study guide project reference • 19 oct 2026 why split metadata and bytes? [SEP]\n\nPostgreSQL is well suited to relational metadata, ownership, state transitions, and transactional constraints.\nObject storage is well suited to file bytes. The database stores enough information to identify an object and decide\nwho may access it, while the object store stores the content itself.Important consistency boundary\nPostgreSQL and S3 do not participate in one shared ACID transaction in this architecture. A database rollback\ncannot automatically undo an S3 upload. The application therefore uses a compensating action: if database\npersistence fails after upload, attempt to delete the uploaded object.\n\nThe logical pipeline is not all implemented yet. The upload/document-management side is built and tested; the\ndurable job-claiming mechanism is being developed; extraction, chunk persistence, embeddings, retrieval, and\nLLM answers remain future work or active next steps.\n\nUse repository + latest tests as source of truth\nPage 6\n\nCONTEXTVAULT • ENGINEERING STUDY GUIDE\nProject reference • 19 Oct 2026\n\n3. Document management and object storage\n\nThe document-management phase established the core file lifecycle. The supported formats recorded in the\nproject are PDF, DOCX, TXT, and Markdown. The upload limit discussed is 5 MB. The backend stores file bytes in\nS3-compatible object storage and document metadata in PostgreSQL.\n\nUpload flow\n\nRecorded upload and job-creation flow\n\nReceive upload\n\nAuthenticated request; multipart file\n\nValidate\n\nExtension/content policy and 5 MB size cap\n\nWrite object\n\nUpload raw bytes to S3-compatible storage\n\nBegin DB transaction\n\nCreate document metadata; flush to obtain ID\n\nCreate pending job\n\nInsert ingestion job in same DB transaction\n\nCommit\n\nDocument and job become durable together\n\nWhy flush before inserting the job?\n\nThe job needs the new document's primary key as its foreign key. SQLAlchemy flushes pending ORM changes to\nthe database within the current transaction and can populate generated IDs. A flush is not a commit: the\ntransaction can still be rolled back. This allows the document row and job row to be committed as one relational\nunit.\n\nUse repository + latest tests as source of truth\nPage 12\n\n", token_count=344, [SEP]\n\nUse repository + latest tests as source of truth\nPage 18CONTEXTVAULT • ENGINEERING STUDY GUIDE\nProject reference • 19 Oct 2026\n\n10. Durable ingestion queue\n\nAn upload endpoint should not need to extract a PDF, parse a DOCX, split text, and compute embeddings before\nresponding. Those operations can be slow and may fail independently. The queue lets the API persist the user's\nupload and the intention to process it, then lets a separate worker perform the work asynchronously.\n\nWhy PostgreSQL-backed queue?\n\n• The project already depends on PostgreSQL, so job state can live alongside document metadata.\n\n• A durable job row survives API process restarts.\n\n• Constraints and transactions can enforce job/document integrity.\n\n• The worker can poll for jobs and claim them safely with PostgreSQL row locks.\n\n• A separate worker process keeps heavy processing out of HTTP request handlers.\n\nTradeoffs\n\nA database queue avoids introducing another service at this stage, but it means the team must deliberately\nimplement polling, backoff, stale-job recovery, lease renewal, retry policy, and observability. It is a practical design\nfor the current project's scale and learning goals; it is not a claim that PostgreSQL is always the best queue for\nevery workload.\n\nJob state model\n\nSimplified lifecycle; retry transitions may return processing to pending\n\nPENDING\n\nWaiting to be claimed\n\nPROCESSING\n\nClaimed by a worker; lease active\n\nCOMPLETED\n\nProcessing succeeded\n\nFAILED\n\nTerminal failure or retries exhausted\n\n", token_count=346, sources=[ChunkSource(source_location=SourceLocation(kind='block', index=1), origin_field='native_text', start_char=0, end_char=70, page_number=19, pdf_metadata=PDFBlockMetaData(block_index=1, bbox=(59.52756118774414, 21.7012939453125, 535.748046875, 32.0662956237793), lines=[PDFLineMetadata(line_index=1, text='CONTEXTVAULT • ENGINEERING STUDY GUIDE', bbox=(59.52756118774414, 21.738792419433594, 233.0025634765625, 32.0662956237793), font_size=7.5, bold_ratio=1.0), PDFLineMetadata(line_index=2, text='Project reference • 19 Oct 2026', bbox=(430.9880065917969, 21.7012939453125, 535.748046875, 32.00629425048828), font_size=7.5, bold_ratio=0.0)])), ChunkSource(source_location=SourceLocation(kind='block', index=2), origin_field='native_text', start_char=0, end_char=27, page_number=19, pdf_metadata=PDFBlockMetaData(block_index=2, bbox=(65.52755737304688, 64.05758666992188, 345.6044921875, 92.9745864868164), lines=[PDFLineMetadata(line_index=1, text='10. A temporary object-store timeout may be worth\nretrying; a corrupt file may fail consistently and should not be retried indefinitely. The policy and status transitions\nneed to be implemented explicitly, not inferred from attempt_count alone.Use repository + latest tests as source of truth\nPage 19\n\nCONTEXTVAULT • ENGINEERING STUDY GUIDE\nProject reference • 19 Oct 2026\n\n11. Job claiming, transactions, and leases\n\nThe current claim service selects the oldest eligible pending job, orders deterministically by created_at and id,\nlimits the query to one row, and uses SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED. If a job is found, it marks it\nprocessing, increments attempt_count, assigns a new UUID claim token, records started_at, and sets a lease\nexpiry approximately seven minutes in the future.\n\nWhat SKIP LOCKED does\n\nSuppose worker A locks the oldest pending job. Worker B runs the same query while A still holds the row lock.\nInstead of waiting for A to release the lock, B skips that row and may claim another eligible row. The project discussions referenced approximately 1 MB chunks at an earlier design point; that size\nshould be revisited before implementation because it is unusually large for typical semantic retrieval chunks. A\nmore conventional chunking policy is usually expressed in tokens or characters and tuned to the embedding\nmodel and document types. Do not blindly carry forward an early size assumption without validating it.The important design properties are: keep chunks small enough to retrieve focused evidence; avoid cutting every\nsentence in half; preserve document ID and source location; define deterministic chunk IDs or a versioning\nstrategy; and avoid exposing chunks from a document whose new ingestion attempt has not fully succeeded.\n\nEmbeddings\n\nAn embedding model maps a text chunk to a numerical vector. Similarity between a question vector and chunk\nvectors can help retrieve semantically relevant passages even when the wording differs. The embedding model,\nvector dimension, normalization policy, and version should be recorded consistently because changing the model\ncan require re-embedding the corpus.\n\nRetrieval\n\nAt query time, the system embeds the user's question, searches the vector index for relevant chunks, and applies\naccess control so the user can only retrieve content they are allowed to see. Reliability and production concerns\n\nThe project is being developed with production-style concerns in mind. The next stage is not just “make parsing\nwork”; it is to make processing recoverable, observable, secure, and safe to retry.\n\nReliability checklist\n\n• Idempotency: retrying a job should not create duplicate active chunks or corrupt a previously successful index.\n\n• Lease recovery: detect jobs left processing after a worker crash and decide when they can be reclaimed.\n\n• Fencing: stale workers must not overwrite the state produced by a newer claim.\n\n• Retry policy: distinguish transient infrastructure errors from deterministic bad-file errors.\n\n• Bounded work: enforce upload limits and avoid unbounded memory usage when reading files.\n\n• Observability: log job ID, document ID, attempt number, duration, failure category, and transition.\n\n• Privacy: avoid logging
